Now I'm considering where to trim space in the document. There's an underfull vbox warning suggesting some wasted whitespace, so I should look for compression opportunities.           " Within-family analysis (Qwen2.5 3B/7B/72B on MMLU) shows qualitatively invariant variance structure: item difficulty dominates at all three scales (73--83\\%, all $G > 0.95$) with consistent facet ranking. The proportional increase at 72B is consistent with the Bernoulli variance ceiling: total variance (0.113) matches $p(1{-}p)$ at grand mean 0.87, compressing all components while item difficulty---the hardest to eliminate---retains the largest share. A crossed 3B$\\times$7B$\\times$72B design yields model$\\times$item = 38.5\\% $>$ item = 35.7\\%, reflecting expected DIF amplification across a 68--87\\% accuracy range ($G_\\text{item} = 0.720$; three models insufficient). The out-of-sample 72B provides preliminary within-family evidence that variance structure is stable across a 24$\\times$ parameter range within Qwen2.5; cross-family generalization remains untested.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Robustness checks.} Three independent MMLU resamples yield CV = 3.1\\%; treating models as fixed increases $G$ by 0.077--0.113 (mean $+0.093$), confirming random-model budgets are conservative (\\S\\ref{sec:model_facet}). Seven additional analyses in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness} confirm stability.",
          "\\ No newline at end of file",
          "+\\paragraph{Robustness checks.} Three MMLU resamples yield CV = 3.1\\%; treating models as fixed increases $G$ by 0.077--0.113, confirming conservative budgets (\\S\\ref{sec:model_facet}; seven additional analyses in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}).",
          "\\ No newline at end of file"
        ]
      }
    ],

```text
85	\subsection{Design-Dependent Reproducibility Budgets}
86	
87	How many items does a given evaluation design need to achieve a target reliability? We translate variance components into item budgets via D-study projections.
88	
89	Table~\ref{tab:dstudy} compares evaluation designs at the $G \geq 0.80$ threshold using single-model (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) MMLU variance components. The multi-facet design (1,296 conditions per item) achieves $G = 0.936$ at 200 items and requires only 55 items for $G \geq 0.80$. A single-condition design reaches only $G = 0.592$ at 200 items and requires 552 items---a 10.0$\times$ budget ratio stable across subset sizes (CV = 1.9\%).
90	
91	\begin{table}[t]
92	\centering
93	\caption{D-study reproducibility budgets under different evaluation designs (single-model, MMLU). Total evals = items $\times$ conditions.}
94	\label{tab:dstudy}
95	\scriptsize
96	\begin{tabular}{lcrrrc}
97	\toprule
98	Design & Cond. & $n_{G{\geq}.80}$ & Total & $G_{200}$ & $G_{116}$ \\
99	\midrule
100	Multi-facet & 1{,}296 & \textbf{55} & 71{,}280 & \textbf{.936} & --- \\
101	Single-cond. & 1 & 552 & 552 & .592 & .457 \\
102	IRT+multi & 1{,}296 & --- & 150{,}336 & --- & .895$^\ddagger$ \\
103	\midrule
104	IRT baseline & --- & 116 & 116 & --- & .457$^\dagger$ \\
105	\bottomrule
106	\end{tabular}
107	
108	\vspace{2pt}
109	{\scriptsize $^\dagger$\,PSN-IRT 116 items \cite{zhou2026irt} under single-condition: $G = 0.457$. $^\ddagger$\,Same 116 items under multi-facet: $G = 0.895$.}
110	\end{table}
111	
112	\citet{zhou2026irt}'s IRT-selected 116 items yield $G = 0.457$ under single-condition but $G = 0.895$ under multi-facet conditions, demonstrating that IRT item selection and G-theory protocol optimization are orthogonal and complementary. An internal consistency check using four-model subsets confirms projection stability ($|\Delta G| \leq 0.029$ for MC, $\leq 0.065$ for FF; Table~\ref{tab:forward_pred}).
113	
114	For practitioners, the design choice depends on the evaluation bottleneck: the full 1,296-condition design minimizes items (55 items, 71,280 evaluations per model); the recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design balances cost and reliability (178 items, 1,602 evaluations); single-condition designs require 552 items at minimal per-item cost. Multi-facet designs suit settings where item construction is expensive (e.g., expert-crafted benchmarks); more items under fewer conditions suit compute-constrained evaluation. A full adoption gradient is in Appendix~\ref{app:intermediate_dstudy}. Cross-model projections show budget ratios of 11--22$\times$ across benchmarks (bootstrap CIs all exceed 10$\times$; Appendix~\ref{app:dstudy_cross}), with multi-facet budgets ranging from 71 to 162 items (Table~\ref{tab:dstudy_cross}). Figure~\ref{fig:teaser} visualizes the D-study curves.
115	
116	\paragraph{Projection assumptions.} Cross-model budgets increase to 93 items because model$\times$item interaction enters relative error variance. Bootstrap CIs (71--162 items across benchmarks) capture item-level variability; model-level stability is assessed via LOMO (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness}). All projections assume the 2024--2025 model generation; the 72B extension (\S4.6) provides preliminary out-of-sample support.
117	
118	\subsection{Preliminary Scale Validation}
119	
120	Within-family analysis (Qwen2.5 3B/7B/72B on MMLU) shows qualitatively invariant variance structure: item difficulty dominates at all three scales (73--83\%, all $G > 0.95$) with consistent facet ranking. The proportional increase at 72B is consistent with the Bernoulli variance ceiling: total variance (0.113) matches $p(1{-}p)$ at grand mean 0.87, compressing all components while item difficulty---the hardest to eliminate---retains the largest share. A crossed 3B$\times$7B$\times$72B design yields model$\times$item = 38.5\% $>$ item = 35.7\%, reflecting expected DIF amplification across a 68--87\% accuracy range ($G_\text{item} = 0.720$; three models insufficient). The out-of-sample 72B provides preliminary within-family evidence that variance structure is stable across a 24$\times$ parameter range within Qwen2.5; cross-family generalization remains untested.
121	
122	\paragraph{Robustness checks.} Three MMLU resamples yield CV = 3.1\%; treating models as fixed increases $G$ by 0.077--0.113, confirming conservative budgets (\S\ref{sec:model_facet}; seven additional analyses in Appendix~\ref{app:robustness}).
```

      "content": "\\subsection{Design-Dependent Reproducibility Budgets}\n\nHow many items does a given evaluation design need to achieve a target reliability? We translate variance components into item budgets via D-study projections.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dstudy} compares evaluation designs at the $G \\geq 0.80$ threshold using single-model (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) MMLU variance components. The multi-facet design (1,296 conditions per item) achieves $G = 0.936$ at 200 items and requires only 55 items for $G \\geq 0.80$. A single-condition design reaches only $G = 0.592$ at 200 items and requires 552 items---a 10.0$\\times$ budget ratio stable across subset sizes (CV = 1.9\\%).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{D-study reproducibility budgets under different evaluation designs (single-model, MMLU). Total evals = items $\\times$ conditions.}\n\\label{tab:dstudy}\n\\scriptsize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcrrrc}\n\\toprule\nDesign & Cond. & $n_{G{\\geq}.80}$ & Total & $G_{200}$ & $G_{116}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nMulti-facet & 1{,}296 & \\textbf{55} & 71{,}280 & \\textbf{.936} & --- \\\\\nSingle-cond. & 1 & 552 & 552 & .592 & .457 \\\\\nIRT+multi & 1{,}296 & --- & 150{,}336 & --- & .895$^\\ddagger$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nIRT baseline & --- & 116 & 116 & --- & .457$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\scriptsize $^\\dagger$\\,PSN-IRT 116 items \\cite{zhou2026irt} under single-condition: $G = 0.457$. $^\\ddagger$\\,Same 116 items under multi-facet: $G = 0.895$.}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\citet{zhou2026irt}'s IRT-selected 116 items yield $G = 0.457$ under single-condition but $G = 0.895$ under multi-facet conditions, demonstrating that IRT item selection and G-theory protocol optimization are orthogonal and complementary. An internal consistency check using four-model subsets confirms projection stability ($|\\Delta G| \\leq 0.029$ for MC, $\\leq 0.065$ for FF; Table~\\ref{tab:forward_pred}).\n\nFor practitioners, the design choice depends on the evaluation bottleneck: the full 1,296-condition design minimizes items (55 items, 71,280 evaluations per model); the recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design balances cost and reliability (178 items, 1,602 evaluations); single-condition designs require 552 items at minimal per-item cost. Multi-facet designs suit settings where item construction is expensive (e.g., expert-crafted benchmarks); more items under fewer conditions suit compute-constrained evaluation. A full adoption gradient is in Appendix~\\ref{app:intermediate_dstudy}. Cross-model projections show budget ratios of 11--22$\\times$ across benchmarks (bootstrap CIs all exceed 10$\\times$; Appendix~\\ref{app:dstudy_cross}), with multi-facet budgets ranging from 71 to 162 items (Table~\\ref{tab:dstudy_cross}). Figure~\\ref{fig:teaser} visualizes the D-study curves.\n\n\\paragraph{Projection assumptions.} Cross-model budgets increase to 93 items because model$\\times$item interaction enters relative error variance. Bootstrap CIs (71--162 items across benchmarks) capture item-level variability; model-level stability is assessed via LOMO (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}). All projections assume the 2024--2025 model generation; the 72B extension (\\S4.6) provides preliminary out-of-sample support.\n\n\\subsection{Preliminary Scale Validation}\n\nWithin-family analysis (Qwen2.5 3B/7B/72B on MMLU) shows qualitatively invariant variance structure: item difficulty dominates at all three scales (73--83\\%, all $G > 0.95$) with consistent facet ranking. The proportional increase at 72B is consistent with the Bernoulli variance ceiling: total variance (0.113) matches $p(1{-}p)$ at grand mean 0.87, compressing all components while item difficulty---the hardest to eliminate---retains the largest share. A crossed 3B$\\times$7B$\\times$72B design yields model$\\times$item = 38.5\\% $>$ item = 35.7\\%, reflecting expected DIF amplification across a 68--87\\% accuracy range ($G_\\text{item} = 0.720$; three models insufficient). The out-of-sample 72B provides preliminary within-family evidence that variance structure is stable across a 24$\\times$ parameter range within Qwen2.5; cross-family generalization remains untested.\n\n\\paragraph{Robustness checks.} Three MMLU resamples yield CV = 3.1\\%; treating models as fixed increases $G$ by 0.077--0.113, confirming conservative budgets (\\S\\ref{sec:model_facet}; seven additional analyses in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}).",
      "numLines": 38,
      "startLine": 85,
      "totalLines": 122
    }
  },

```text
1	\appendix
2	\section{Additional Robustness Analyses}
3	\label{app:robustness}
4	
5	We report nine robustness analyses that verify the stability of the variance decomposition under alternative analytical choices.
6	
7	\paragraph{GLMM complement.} A generalized linear mixed model (GLMM) with marginal logit link produces variance component rankings that perfectly preserve all seven grouped component orderings ($\rho = 1.0$) relative to Henderson Method~I \cite{jiang2018bayesian, tuerlinckx2006glmm}. This confirms that the linear variance decomposition is robust to the binary outcome distribution.
8	
9	\paragraph{Accuracy-range stability.} The \texttt{item\_id} variance share remains stable across subsets with mean accuracy from 0.62 to 0.72 ($r = -0.048$). This rules out distortion from binary floor or ceiling effects at observed accuracy levels.
10	
11	\paragraph{Estimator robustness.} We compare Henderson Method~I with restricted maximum likelihood (REML-EM, 500 iterations, practical convergence $\Delta < 0.003$ percentage points). All variance components differ by $<$0.5 percentage points between estimators. The largest discrepancy occurs on the residual component (0.47pp); all substantive components (item, model$\times$item, model) differ by $<$0.3pp. Henderson Method~I is preferred for our balanced designs because it produces unbiased estimates without distributional assumptions \cite[Ch.~12]{brennan2001generalizability}.
12	
13	\paragraph{Probit simulation.} A probit simulation matched to our 1,296-condition design confirms that Henderson Method~I recovers variance component rankings from dichotomized data (Spearman $\rho = 0.732$ between binary and continuous rankings, excluding residual; \texttt{item\_id} dominant in all 100 replications; top-5 ordering correct in 79\% of replications). Bias analysis by difficulty stratum shows $|\text{bias}| = 0.28$--$0.37$ for items with $p \in [0.5, 0.8]$, where the Bernoulli variance ceiling $p(1{-}p)$ is largest. This analysis covers the observed accuracy range ($p \in [0.3, 0.9]$); extreme-difficulty items ($p < 0.3$ or $p > 0.9$) are rare in our sample ($<$8\% of items) and may exhibit larger deviations. Henderson Method~I absorbs this Bernoulli-inherent variance into the residual, explaining inflated bias near medium difficulty; systematic component ordering is robustly recovered across all replications. Binary scoring inflates residual variance by absorbing the Bernoulli $p(1{-}p)$ ceiling, which enters $\sigma^2_\text{rel}$ and increases D-study item requirements. D-study budgets derived from binary data are therefore conservative: continuous-scored designs would require equal or fewer items for the same $G$ threshold. Null calibration (ratio $= 1.0008$) confirms that this conservatism does not distort relative component magnitudes.
14	
15	\paragraph{CI method comparison.} Bootstrap (1,000 item-level resamples with replacement) and delete-1 jackknife confidence intervals agree closely: width ratios range from 0.96 to 1.04 across all variance components. Bootstrap CIs capture item-sampling variability conditional on these 8 specific models; model-level stability is assessed separately via LOMO (below). The agreement indicates that the dependence structure introduced by item-level bootstrap resampling (which creates $\sim$127 unique items per 200-item resample) does not materially distort interval estimates. Stratified resampling by subject (10 subjects $\times$ 20 items) produces CIs within 4\% of simple item-level bootstrap, confirming that the within-subject dependence structure does not materially affect interval estimates.
16	
17	\paragraph{Facet specification sensitivity.} Treating temperature and ordering as fixed (rather than random) facets changes $G_\text{item}$ by only 0.37 percentage points (all-random: 0.820 vs.\ fixed temperature+ordering: 0.824). The small difference reflects the negligible temperature$\times$item (0.65\%) and ordering$\times$item (0.54\%) variance shares. The random-facet assumption is therefore robust to facet classification.
18	
19	\paragraph{Leave-one-model-out (LOMO).} Across all 40 leave-one-model-out subsets (8 models $\times$ 5 benchmarks), $G_\text{item}$ ranges from 0.833 to 0.919, with CV $\leq$ 1.25\% per benchmark. No single model removal changes $G$ by more than 0.030 (GSM8K, removing Gemma-2-9B). Item-related variance rankings are preserved in all 40 subsets. Table~\ref{tab:lomo_full} reports per-benchmark stability. This supports treating models as exchangeable within the target universe of 7--9B instruction-tuned models (\S\ref{sec:model_facet}).
20	
21	\begin{table}[h]
22	\centering
23	\caption{LOMO stability across five benchmarks (8 models $\times$ 5 benchmarks = 40 subsets).}
24	\label{tab:lomo_full}
25	\scriptsize
26	\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
27	\toprule
28	Benchmark & $G_\text{full}$ & CV (\%) & Max $|\Delta G|$ & Most impactful \\
29	\midrule
30	MMLU & 0.896 & 0.49 & 0.019 & InternLM2.5-7B \\
31	ARC & 0.892 & 0.94 & 0.026 & Llama-3.1-8B \\
32	HellaSwag & 0.872 & 1.09 & 0.028 & Llama-3.1-8B \\
33	GSM8K & 0.863 & 1.25 & 0.030 & Gemma-2-9B \\
34	MATH & 0.888 & 1.20 & 0.029 & InternLM2.5-7B \\
35	\bottomrule
36	\end{tabular}
37	\end{table}
38	
39	\paragraph{Inter-model agreement.} We computed pairwise Cohen's $\kappa$ and Fleiss' $\kappa$ across all model pairs on each benchmark. Mean pairwise $\kappa$ ranges from 0.326 (MATH) to 0.461 (MMLU); Fleiss' $\kappa$ ranges from 0.309 (GSM8K) to 0.456 (MMLU). HellaSwag uses six models ($\kappa = 0.332$) due to missing OLMo and Yi data. The fair-to-moderate agreement independently confirms that the model$\times$item interaction (30--36\% of variance) reflects genuine differential item functioning: models disagree substantially on which items are difficult, consistent with prior DIF findings in educational measurement \cite{holland1993dif, clauser1998dif}.
40	
41	\paragraph{Scoring metric sensitivity.}
42	\label{sec:partial_credit}
43	To probe metric conditionality, we compared binary correctness with step-level partial credit on GSM8K (200 items, 288 conditions, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct). Prompt template variance drops from 25.5\% (binary) to 1.2\% (partial credit), while residual rises from 28.2\% to 46.0\%. Item reliability decreases ($G_\text{item} = 0.553$ vs.\ $0.759$), and item difficulty rankings change substantially ($\rho = 0.26$). Binary scoring amplifies prompt sensitivity by collapsing reasoning quality into pass/fail, confirming that all findings in this paper are metric-conditional.
44	
45	\paragraph{Facet specification sensitivity: random vs.\ fixed model.} Table~\ref{tab:fixed_vs_random} reports per-benchmark generalizability coefficients under random and fixed model specifications. The consistent $\Delta G$ of 0.077--0.113 confirms that random-model budgets are strictly more conservative across all benchmarks.
46	
47	\begin{table}[h]
48	\centering
49	\caption{Generalizability coefficients under random vs.\ fixed model facet specification across five benchmarks.}
50	\label{tab:fixed_vs_random}

The largest discrepancy occurs on the residual component (0.47pp); all substantive components (item, model$\\times$item, model) differ by $<$0.3pp. Henderson Method~I is preferred for our balanced designs because it produces unbiased estimates without distributional assumptions \\cite[Ch.~12]{brennan2001generalizability}.\n\n\\paragraph{Probit simulation.} A probit simulation matched to our 1,296-condition design confirms that Henderson Method~I recovers variance component rankings from dichotomized data (Spearman $\\rho = 0.732$ between binary and continuous rankings, excluding residual; \\texttt{item\\_id} dominant in all 100 replications; top-5 ordering correct in 79\\% of replications). Bias analysis by difficulty stratum shows $|\\text{bias}| = 0.28$--$0.37$ for items with $p \\in [0.5, 0.8]$, where the Bernoulli variance ceiling $p(1{-}p)$ is largest. This analysis covers the observed accuracy range ($p \\in [0.3, 0.9]$); extreme-difficulty items ($p < 0.3$ or $p > 0.9$) are rare in our sample ($<$8\\% of items) and may exhibit larger deviations. Henderson Method~I absorbs this Bernoulli-inherent variance into the residual, explaining inflated bias near medium difficulty; systematic component ordering is robustly recovered across all replications. Binary scoring inflates residual variance by absorbing the Bernoulli $p(1{-}p)$ ceiling, which enters $\\sigma^2_\\text{rel}$ and increases D-study item requirements. D-study budgets derived from binary data are therefore conservative: continuous-scored designs would require equal or fewer items for the same $G$ threshold. Null calibration (ratio $= 1.0008$) confirms that this conservatism does not distort relative component magnitudes.\n\n\\paragraph{CI method comparison.} Bootstrap (1,000 item-level resamples with replacement) and delete-1 jackknife confidence intervals agree closely: width ratios range from 0.96 to 1.04 across all variance components. Bootstrap CIs capture item-sampling variability conditional on these 8 specific models; model-level stability is assessed separately via LOMO (below). The agreement indicates that the dependence structure introduced by item-level bootstrap resampling (which creates $\\sim$127 unique items per 200-item resample) does not materially distort interval estimates. Stratified resampling by subject (10 subjects $\\times$ 20 items) produces CIs within 4\\% of simple item-level bootstrap, confirming that the within-subject dependence structure does not materially affect interval estimates.\n\n\\paragraph{Facet specification sensitivity.} Treating temperature and ordering as fixed (rather than random) facets changes $G_\\text{item}$ by only 0.37 percentage points (all-random: 0.820 vs.\\ fixed temperature+ordering: 0.824). The small difference reflects the negligible temperature$\\times$item (0.65\\%) and ordering$\\times$item (0.54\\%) variance shares. The random-facet assumption is therefore robust to facet classification.\n\n\\paragraph{Leave-one-model-out (LOMO).} Across all 40 leave-one-model-out subsets (8 models $\\times$ 5 benchmarks), $G_\\text{item}$ ranges from 0.833 to 0.919, with CV $\\leq$ 1.25\\% per benchmark. No single model removal changes $G$ by more than 0.030 (GSM8K, removing Gemma-2-9B). Item-related variance rankings are preserved in all 40 subsets. Table~\\ref{tab:lomo_full} reports per-benchmark stability. This supports treating models as exchangeable within the target universe of 7--9B instruction-tuned models (\\S\\ref{sec:model_facet}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{LOMO stability across five benchmarks (8 models $\\times$ 5 benchmarks = 40 subsets).}\n\\label{tab:lomo_full}\n\\scriptsize\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & $G_\\text{full}$ & CV (\\%) & Max $|\\Delta G|$ & Most impactful \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & 0.896 & 0.49 & 0.019 & InternLM2.5-7B \\\\\nARC & 0.892 & 0.94 & 0.026 & Llama-3.1-8B \\\\\nHellaSwag & 0.872 & 1.09 & 0.028 & Llama-3.1-8B \\\\\nGSM8K & 0.863 & 1.25 & 0.030 & Gemma-2-9B \\\\\nMATH & 0.888 & 1.20 & 0.029 & InternLM2.5-7B \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Inter-model agreement.} We computed pairwise Cohen's $\\kappa$ and Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across all model pairs on each benchmark. Mean pairwise $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.326 (MATH) to 0.461 (MMLU); Fleiss' $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.309 (GSM8K) to 0.456 (MMLU). HellaSwag uses six models ($\\kappa = 0.332$) due to missing OLMo and Yi data. The fair-to-moderate agreement independently confirms that the model$\\times$item interaction (30--36\\% of variance) reflects genuine differential item functioning: models disagree substantially on which items are difficult, consistent with prior DIF findings in educational measurement \\cite{holland1993dif, clauser1998dif}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scoring metric sensitivity.}\n\\label{sec:partial_credit}\nTo probe metric conditionality, we compared binary correctness with step-level partial credit on GSM8K (200 items, 288 conditions, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct). Prompt template variance drops from 25.5\\% (binary) to 1.2\\% (partial credit), while residual rises from 28.2\\% to 46.0\\%. Item reliability decreases ($G_\\text{item} = 0.553$ vs.\\ $0.759$), and item difficulty rankings change substantially ($\\rho = 0.26$). Binary scoring amplifies prompt sensitivity by collapsing reasoning quality into pass/fail, confirming that all findings in this paper are metric-conditional.\n\n\\paragraph{Facet specification sensitivity: random vs.\\ fixed model.} Table~\\ref{tab:fixed_vs_random} reports per-benchmark generalizability coefficients under random and fixed model specifications. The consistent $\\Delta G$ of 0.077--0.113 confirms that random-model budgets are strictly more conservative across all benchmarks.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Generalizability coefficients under random vs.\\ fixed model facet specification across five benchmarks.}\n\\label{tab:fixed_vs_random}",

41	\subsection{Exploratory: Format-Associated Variance Patterns}
42	\label{sec:format_conditional}
43	
44	We extend to five benchmarks (three MC: MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag; two FF: GSM8K, MATH) using exp-002 (8 models $\times$ BF16 $\times$ 2 temperatures $\times$ 6 prompts $\times$ 6 seeds $\times$ 4 orderings $\times$ 200 items). Table~\ref{tab:cross_benchmark} reveals a format-associated pattern. For all three MC benchmarks, item-related variance (item + model$\times$item DIF; e.g., MMLU: 37.9\% + 30.2\% = 68.1\%) concentrates at 68--72\%. HellaSwag is the sole MC case where model$\times$item slightly exceeds item (36.0\% vs.\ 34.3\%), though their sum remains in the MC range. Among MC benchmarks, ARC-Challenge shows an anomalously high model$\times$prompt interaction (8.0\% vs.\ $<$2\% for MMLU and HellaSwag), suggesting that science-domain items are differentially sensitive to option formatting across model families.
45	
46	Free-form benchmarks differ markedly. On GSM8K, variance distributes across item (16.9\%), model$\times$item (14.6\%), model (10.7\%), and model$\times$prompt (8.5\%); prompt main effect is 5.4\%. MATH shows an intermediate pattern: item leads at 24.0\% with model$\times$item at 17.3\%, but residual variance is substantially larger (43.9\%). Item-level regression reveals that FF residual variance is predominantly explained by the Bernoulli variance ceiling $p(1{-}p)$ ($R^2 = 0.47$--$0.86$; Appendix~\ref{app:ff_residual}): medium-difficulty items maximize binary outcome variance regardless of facet structure, so the elevated FF residuals reflect measurement resolution rather than unmodeled substantive sources. All format-associated findings are conditional on binary scoring (\S\ref{sec:binary}), given that scoring granularity reshapes variance structure (binary $G = 0.936$ vs.\ text-exact-match $G = 0.007$; \S4.1).

69	
70	MC benchmarks concentrate item-related variance at 68--72\%, while FF benchmarks disperse it to 31--41\% ($n = 5$; permutation $p = 0.10$; item-level $d = 1.73$, benchmark-level CI spans zero); we report this as a descriptive observation. This does not extend to item budgets: HellaSwag (MC, 118 items for $G \geq 0.80$) exceeds ARC (98) and MATH (101), showing format alone does not predict evaluation cost (Figure~\ref{fig:cross_benchmark}). The factorial design is not fully symmetric: ordering applies only to MC, and prompt templates serve format-specific functions, potentially confounding format comparisons.
71	
72	Difficulty dispersion ($\text{std}(p_i)$) strongly predicts item variance share ($\rho = 0.82$, $n = 5$ benchmarks; Appendix~\ref{app:difficulty_scatter}; reasoning-heavy subjects show $G_\text{item}$ as low as 0.614, Appendix~\ref{app:subject_gstudy}). Format constrains the noise floor, but difficulty heterogeneity determines variance magnitude within it.

Free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) distribute variance across item, model, and interaction components with no single dominant source.}

81	\subsection{Precision Separability}
82	
83	Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 are functionally interchangeable ($<$0.1\% interaction \cite{yuan2025layercast}); under text-exact-match, a precision$\times$temperature interaction emerges at 1.3\%, consistent with \S4.1. Precision need not be controlled for binary evaluation at BF16/FP16 \cite{shanmugavelu2024floating}.
84

67	\paragraph{Null calibration.} We generate 100 Bernoulli-distributed datasets with item difficulties matched to the empirical distribution but no systematic facet effects. The observed-to-null variance ratio is 1.0008, confirming that Henderson Method~I does not introduce systematic bias when applied to binary outcomes. The null simulation also verifies that the item variance share (58.4\%) is not an artifact of the Bernoulli $p(1{-}p)$ ceiling: the null produces item variance of 0.08\%, three orders of magnitude below the observed value. Across all 100 replications, the same three components (item, model$\times$item, residual) emerge as the top three (set consistency 100\%), with Kendall's $\tau = 0.81$ between binary and continuous rankings.
68	
69	\paragraph{Reduced crossing validation.} To verify that the reduced crossing in exp-002 (BF16 only $\times$ 2 temperatures) does not introduce confounding, we extracted the corresponding subset from the fully crossed exp-001 design. The generalizability coefficient differs by only 0.0008 ($G = 0.9361$ vs.\ $0.9369$), and all non-precision variance components differ by less than 2 percentage points. The 10pp increase in item variance share (58.4\% $\to$ 68.5\%) is fully explained by the removal of precision-related components ($\sim$4.5\% total), which redistributes across remaining sources. This confirms that the exp-002 design preserves the variance structure of the full crossing.
70	
71	\section{Cross-Model D-Study Projections}
72	\label{app:dstudy_cross}
73	
74	Table~\ref{tab:dstudy_cross} extends the single-model D-study (Table~\ref{tab:dstudy} in main text) to per-benchmark cross-model projections with bootstrap confidence intervals (1,000 item-level resamples, 8 models, 2,304 conditions per item). The efficiency ratio is format-associated: MC benchmarks show 11--13$\times$ (mean 12.0$\times$ [11.6, 12.5]), while FF benchmarks show 21--22$\times$ (mean 21.5$\times$ [20.4, 22.8]).
75

101	The higher item requirements relative to the single-model analysis (55 items in Table~\ref{tab:dstudy}) reflect the additional model$\times$item interaction variance that enters $\sigma^2_\text{rel}$ when models are crossed as a facet. The format-associated ratio pattern---FF benchmarks showing $\sim$2$\times$ the MC ratio---is consistent with the distributed variance structure observed in \S\ref{sec:format_conditional}, where FF benchmarks allocate more variance to model and model$\times$prompt components.
102	
103	\paragraph{Internal consistency check.} Table~\ref{tab:forward_pred} reports consistency checks from four exp-001 models (DeepSeek, Llama, Mistral, Qwen3) to the full eight-model design across all five benchmarks. MC benchmarks show $|\Delta G| \leq 0.029$ ($<$3.5\% error), while FF benchmarks show larger deviations ($|\Delta G| \leq 0.065$, $\sim$7\%) due to limited model diversity in the four-model subset. FF predictions consistently underestimate actual $G$ (conservative); MC predictions show mixed directions with small deviations ($|\Delta G| \leq 0.029$).

130	Figure~\ref{fig:budget_sensitivity} shows the effect of $|\Delta G| = 0.035$ estimation error on item budgets across all five benchmarks. The percentage increase is remarkably stable (23.0--24.5\%, mean 23.2\%), because the relative budget adjustment $\Delta n / n$ depends on the $G$-formula ratio rather than on absolute variance components. Absolute $\Delta n$ ranges from 13 (ARC) to 67 (GSM8K), scaling with the baseline budget.

136	\label{fig:budget_sensitivity}
137	\end{figure}
138	
139	\section{Intermediate D-Study Designs}
140	\label{app:intermediate_dstudy}
141	
142	Table~\ref{tab:intermediate_dstudy} shows how reliability improves as evaluation designs increase from single-condition to full multi-facet crossing. The steepest gain occurs at the ``minimal upgrade'' level (2 prompts): $G_{200}$ jumps from 0.592 to 0.692 at 2$\times$ cost. A minimal sufficient design for $G \geq 0.80$ is 3 prompts $\times$ 3 seeds (9 conditions, $G_{200} = 0.818$, $n_{G \geq 0.80} = 178$). Beyond the ``standard'' level (32 conditions), adding orderings provides negligible benefit (ordering$\times$item $<$1\%), confirming that prompt and seed diversity are the primary reliability drivers.

164	\section{Cross-Benchmark Difficulty Heterogeneity}
165	\label{app:difficulty_scatter}
166	
167	Figure~\ref{fig:difficulty_scatter} plots item difficulty heterogeneity ($\text{std}(p_i)$ across models) against \texttt{item\_id} variance share for all five benchmarks. MC benchmarks cluster at higher dispersion ($0.27$--$0.31$) and higher item variance ($34$--$38\%$), while FF benchmarks occupy the lower-left region ($0.22$--$0.23$, $17$--$24\%$). The cross-benchmark Spearman $\rho = 0.82$ ($p = 0.089$, $n = 5$) is consistent in direction with the within-MMLU correlation ($\rho = 0.806$, $p = 0.005$, $n = 10$ subjects); these are independent analyses at different granularities (benchmarks vs.\ subjects within MMLU), providing multi-scale support for the item difficulty heterogeneity mechanism discussed in \S\ref{sec:format_conditional}.

MC benchmarks (filled) exhibit higher difficulty dispersion and item variance than FF benchmarks (open). Spearman $\rho = 0.82$, $p = 0.089$.}
173	\label{fig:difficulty_scatter}
174	\end{figure}

177	\label{app:subject_gstudy}
178	
179	Table~\ref{tab:subject_gstudy} reports per-subject variance decomposition for 10 MMLU subjects (20 items each, 8 models, 2,304 conditions per item). $G_\text{item}$ ranges from 0.614 (\texttt{high\_school\_mathematics}) to 0.930 (\texttt{international\_law}), with mean 0.843. Knowledge-intensive subjects (\texttt{international\_law}, \texttt{clinical\_knowledge}, \texttt{world\_religions}) show \texttt{item\_id} $>$ 40\%, while reasoning-heavy subjects (\texttt{high\_school\_mathematics}, \texttt{abstract\_algebra}) show model$\times$item $>$ \texttt{item\_id}, consistent with models differing more in reasoning strategies than in factual recall. The \texttt{high\_school\_mathematics} \texttt{item\_id} of 10.4\% referenced in \S\ref{sec:format_conditional} is the lowest across all subjects, below even GSM8K (16.9\%).

Spearman $\\rho = 0.82$, $p = 0.089$.}\n\\label{fig:difficulty_scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{MMLU Subject-Level Variance Decomposition}\n\\label{app:subject_gstudy}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:subject_gstudy} reports per-subject variance decomposition for 10 MMLU subjects (20 items each, 8 models, 2,304 conditions per item). $G_\\text{item}$ ranges from 0.614 (\\texttt{high\\_school\\_mathematics}) to 0.930 (\\texttt{international\\_law}), with mean 0.843. Knowledge-intensive subjects (\\texttt{international\\_law}, \\texttt{clinical\\_knowledge}, \\texttt{world\\_religions}) show \\texttt{item\\_id} $>$ 40\\%, while reasoning-heavy subjects (\\texttt{high\\_school\\_mathematics}, \\texttt{abstract\\_algebra}) show model$\\times$item $>$ \\texttt{item\\_id}, consistent with models differing more in reasoning strategies than in factual recall. The \\texttt{high\\_school\\_mathematics} \\texttt{item\\_id} of 10.4\\% referenced in \\S\\ref{sec:format_conditional} is the lowest across all subjects, below even GSM8K (16.9\\%).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Subject-level variance decomposition within MMLU (20 items per subject, 8 models, 2,304 conditions per item). Sorted by $G_\\text{item}$ descending.}\n\\label{tab:subject_gstudy}\n\\scriptsize\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}\n\\toprule\nSubject & Acc. & item\\% & mod.$\\times$it.\\% & $G_\\text{item}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\ninternational\\_law & .717 & 44.4 & 24.1 & .930 \\\\\nclinical\\_knowledge & .581 & 47.5 & 30.0 & .923 \\\\\nworld\\_religions & .826 & 42.2 & 27.1 & .910 \\\\\ncomputer\\_security & .740 & 37.0 & 24.9 & .908 \\\\\ncollege\\_physics & .436 & 31.6 & 30.1 & .876 \\\\\nphilosophy & .782 & 33.0 & 36.1 & .868 \\\\\nabstract\\_algebra & .457 & 24.1 & 32.8 & .822 \\\\\nus\\_foreign\\_policy & .864 & 20.4 & 36.9 & .802 \\\\\nprofessional\\_medicine & .671 & 19.9 & 41.7 & .778 \\\\\nhigh\\_school\\_math & .385 & 10.4 & 41.9 & .614 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & --- & 31.1 & 32.6 & .843 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{FF Residual Variance Analysis}\n\\label{app:ff_residual}\n\nTo investigate whether the elevated residual variance in free-form benchmarks (GSM8K 39.9\\%, MATH 43.9\\%) reflects unmodeled substantive sources or measurement properties of binary scoring, we regress item-level residual variance on item characteristics across all five benchmarks (Table~\\ref{tab:ff_residual}).",

  "old_string": "We extend to five benchmarks (three MC: MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag; two FF: GSM8K, MATH) using exp-002 (8 models $\\times$ BF16 $\\times$ 2 temperatures $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 4 orderings $\\times$ 200 items). Table~\\ref{tab:cross_benchmark} reveals a format-associated pattern. For all three MC benchmarks, item-related variance (item + model$\\times$item DIF; e.g., MMLU: 37.9\\% + 30.2\\% = 68.1\\%) concentrates at 68--72\\%. HellaSwag is the sole MC case where model$\\times$item slightly exceeds item (36.0\\% vs.\\ 34.3\\%), though their sum remains in the MC range. Among MC benchmarks, ARC-Challenge shows an anomalously high model$\\times$prompt interaction (8.0\\% vs.\\ $<$2\\% for MMLU and HellaSwag), suggesting that science-domain items are differentially sensitive to option formatting across model families.\n\nFree-form benchmarks differ markedly. On GSM8K, variance distributes across item (16.9\\%), model$\\times$item (14.6\\%), model (10.7\\%), and model$\\times$prompt (8.5\\%); prompt main effect is 5.4\\%. MATH shows an intermediate pattern: item leads at 24.0\\% with model$\\times$item at 17.3\\%, but residual variance is substantially larger (43.9\\%). Item-level regression reveals that FF residual variance is predominantly explained by the Bernoulli variance ceiling $p(1{-}p)$ ($R^2 = 0.47$--$0.86$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ff_residual}): medium-difficulty items maximize binary outcome variance regardless of facet structure, so the elevated FF residuals reflect measurement resolution rather than unmodeled substantive sources. All format-associated findings are conditional on binary scoring (\\S\\ref{sec:binary}), given that scoring granularity reshapes variance structure (binary $G = 0.936$ vs.\\ text-exact-match $G = 0.007$; \\S4.1).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Format-associated variance structure across five benchmarks (exp-002, 8 models). Variance components (\\%) per benchmark; MC = multiple-choice, FF = free-form. This does not extend to item budgets: HellaSwag (MC, 118 items for $G \\geq 0.80$) exceeds ARC (98) and MATH (101), showing format alone does not predict evaluation cost (Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark}).   "new_string": "\\section{Format-Associated Variance: Per-Benchmark Details}\n\\label{app:format_details}\n\nThis section supplements the compressed format-associated analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:format_conditional} with per-benchmark details.\n\n\\paragraph{MC benchmark exceptions.} HellaSwag is the sole MC case where model$\\times$item slightly exceeds item (36.0\\% vs.\\ 34.3\\%), though their sum (70.3\\%) remains in the MC range (68--72\\%). Among MC benchmarks, ARC-Challenge shows an anomalously high model$\\times$prompt interaction (8.0\\% vs.\\ $<$2\\% for MMLU and HellaSwag), suggesting that science-domain items are differentially sensitive to option formatting across model families.\n\n\\paragraph{FF benchmark patterns.} Free-form benchmarks differ markedly from MC. On GSM8K, variance distributes across item (16.9\\%), model$\\times$item (14.6\\%), model (10.7\\%), and model$\\times$prompt (8.5\\%); prompt main effect is 5.4\\%. MATH shows an intermediate pattern: item leads at 24.0\\% with model$\\times$item at 17.3\\%, but residual variance is substantially larger (43.9\\%). Item-level regression reveals that FF residual variance is predominantly explained by the Bernoulli variance ceiling $p(1{-}p)$ ($R^2 = 0.47$--$0.86$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ff_residual}): medium-difficulty items maximize binary outcome variance regardless of facet structure, so the elevated FF residuals reflect measurement resolution rather than unmodeled substantive sources.\n\n\\paragraph{Difficulty dispersion as variance predictor.} Difficulty dispersion ($\\text{std}(p_i)$) strongly predicts item variance share ($\\rho = 0.82$, $n = 5$ benchmarks; Appendix~\\ref{app:difficulty_scatter}; reasoning-heavy subjects show $G_\\text{item}$ as low as 0.614, Appendix~\\ref{app:subject_gstudy}). Format constrains the noise floor, but difficulty heterogeneity determines variance magnitude within it.\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Difficulty Heterogeneity}\n\\label{app:difficulty_scatter}"
}

    "oldString": "\\section{Cross-Benchmark Difficulty Heterogeneity}\n\\label{app:difficulty_scatter}",
    "newString": "\\section{Format-Associated Variance: Per-Benchmark Details}\n\\label{app:format_details}\n\nThis section supplements the compressed format-associated analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:format_conditional} with per-benchmark details.\n\n\\paragraph{MC benchmark exceptions.} HellaSwag is the sole MC case where model$\\times$item slightly exceeds item (36.0\\% vs.\\ 34.3\\%), though their sum (70.3\\%) remains in the MC range (68--72\\%). Among MC benchmarks, ARC-Challenge shows an anomalously high model$\\times$prompt interaction (8.0\\% vs.\\ $<$2\\% for MMLU and HellaSwag), suggesting that science-domain items are differentially sensitive to option formatting across model families.\n\n\\paragraph{FF benchmark patterns.} Free-form benchmarks differ markedly from MC. On GSM8K, variance distributes across item (16.9\\%), model$\\times$item (14.6\\%), model (10.7\\%), and model$\\times$prompt (8.5\\%); prompt main effect is 5.4\\%. MATH shows an intermediate pattern: item leads at 24.0\\% with model$\\times$item at 17.3\\%, but residual variance is substantially larger (43.9\\%). Item-level regression reveals that FF residual variance is predominantly explained by the Bernoulli variance ceiling $p(1{-}p)$ ($R^2 = 0.47$--$0.86$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ff_residual}): medium-difficulty items maximize binary outcome variance regardless of facet structure, so the elevated FF residuals reflect measurement resolution rather than unmodeled substantive sources.\n\n\\paragraph{Difficulty dispersion as variance predictor.} Difficulty dispersion ($\\text{std}(p_i)$) strongly predicts item variance share ($\\rho = 0.82$, $n = 5$ benchmarks; Appendix~\\ref{app:difficulty_scatter}; reasoning-heavy subjects show $G_\\text{item}$ as low as 0.614, Appendix~\\ref{app:subject_gstudy}). Format constrains the noise floor, but difficulty heterogeneity determines variance magnitude within it.\n\n\\section{Cross-Benchmark Difficulty Heterogeneity}\n\\label{app:difficulty_scatter}",

2	\label{sec:conclusion}

  "content": "[Director 指令] [Patch] Round 10 补充修复 — SF5 压缩 + SF7 结论 + MF3 新数据\n\n**执行顺序**: 先 SF5（释放空间）→ SF7（用释放的空间）→ MF3（一句话即可）\n\n## SF5: §4.3 压缩（优先级最高）\n\n当前 §4.3 \"Exploratory: Format-Associated Variance Patterns\" 正文过长（~1 整页含 table + figure），Reviewer 连续 7 轮指出 n=5 exploratory observation 不值这么多篇幅。\n\n压缩方案：\n- 保留 Table 3 + Figure 1 + 1 段核心 narrative（MC 68-72% vs FF 31-41%, 但 format alone 不决定 budget）\n- 将以下内容移入 Appendix 新 section \"Format-Associated Variance Details\"：\n  - ARC model×prompt anomaly 讨论\n  - HellaSwag model×item > item 例外说明\n  - Difficulty dispersion 预测力细节（ρ=0.82 保留一句引用，详细数据移附录）\n  - Design asymmetry confound 讨论\n- 目标：正文释放 ~0.3 页\n\n## SF7: Conclusion 扩展\n\n当前仅 4 句话。用 SF5 释放的空间增加 2-3 句 practitioner-oriented 内容：\n- G_item 和 G_model 有不同瓶颈（item count vs prompt standardization），提供正交优化路径\n- 对 benchmark designers：multi-facet validation 应成为标准实践\n- 对 leaderboard operators：至少 3-prompt × 3-seed 设计\n\n## MF3: Probit 段落补充 +36% 数据\n\nAppendix probit 段落已有方向性分析（\"D-study budgets derived from binary data are therefore conservative\"）。追加一句具体量化：\n\n\"Within a matched probit simulation, binarization increases D-study budgets by approximately 36\\% (99 $\\to$ 135 items for $G \\geq 0.80$), driven by residual inflation (16\\% $\\to$ 41\\%) and item variance compression (50\\% $\\to$ 32\\%).\"\n\n## G_model 确认\n\n§3.6 当前 G_model 值（MMLU 0.80, ARC 0.52 等）是 3×3 recommended design 下的值，已正确标注 \"under the recommended 3-prompt × 3-seed design with 200 items\"。无需修改。Worker 另计算了 full multi-facet design 值（0.863-0.958），如有空间可在同句补充 \"under the full design, $G_\\text{model}$ rises to 0.67--0.96\"，但非必须。\n\n编译后确认主文 ≤ 8 页。"

**执行顺序**: 先 SF5（释放空间）→ SF7（用释放的空间）→ MF3（一句话即可）

## SF5: §4.3 压缩（优先级最高）

当前 §4.3 "Exploratory: Format-Associated Variance Patterns" 正文过长（~1 整页含 table + figure），Reviewer 连续 7 轮指出 n=5 exploratory observation 不值这么多篇幅。

压缩方案：
- 保留 Table 3 + Figure 1 + 1 段核心 narrative（MC 68-72% vs FF 31-41%, 但 format alone 不决定 budget）
- 将以下内容移入 Appendix 新 section "Format-Associated Variance Details"：
  - ARC model×prompt anomaly 讨论
  - HellaSwag model×item > item 例外说明
  - Difficulty dispersion 预测力细节（ρ=0.82 保留一句引用，详细数据移附录）
  - Design asymmetry confound 讨论
- 目标：正文释放 ~0.3 页

## SF7: Conclusion 扩展

当前仅 4 句话。用 SF5 释放的空间增加 2-3 句 practitioner-oriented 内容：
- G_item 和 G_model 有不同瓶颈（item count vs prompt standardization），提供正交优化路径
- 对 benchmark designers：multi-facet validation 应成为标准实践
- 对 leaderboard operators：至少 3-prompt × 3-seed 设计

## MF3: Probit 段落补充 +36% 数据

Appendix probit 段落已有方向性分析（"D-study budgets derived from binary data are therefore conservative"）。追加一句具体量化：

"Within a matched probit simulation, binarization increases D-study budgets by approximately 36\% (99 $\to$ 135 items for $G \geq 0.80$), driven by residual inflation (16\% $\to$ 41\%) and item variance compression (50\% $\to$ 32\%)."

## G_model 确认

§3.6 当前 G_model 值（MMLU 0.80, ARC 0.52 等）是 3×3 recommended design 下的值，已正确标注 "under the recommended 3-prompt × 3-seed design with 200 items"。无需修改。Worker 另计算了 full multi-facet design 值（0.863-0.958），如有空间可在同句补充 "under the full design, $G_\text{model}$ rises to 0.67--0.96"，但非必须。

编译后确认主文 ≤ 8 页。

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	A recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design reduces item requirements from 552 to 178 for $G \geq 0.80$, confirmed across five benchmarks under binary scoring; multi-facet designs minimize items when item construction is costly, while single-condition designs suit compute-constrained settings. All budgets assume binary scoring; extending to other model scales and continuous metrics is a priority. Benchmark designers can use D-study projections to size pilot studies before committing to full item development; leaderboard operators can improve ranking stability by averaging over a small number of prompt and seed conditions. Code, data, and a D-study calculator will be released upon publication.
```

41	\subsection{Exploratory: Format-Associated Variance Patterns}
42	\label{sec:format_conditional}
43	
44	We extend to five benchmarks (three MC: MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag; two FF: GSM8K, MATH) using exp-002 (8 models $\times$ BF16 $\times$ 2 temperatures $\times$ 6 prompts $\times$ 6 seeds $\times$ 4 orderings $\times$ 200 items). Table~\ref{tab:cross_benchmark} reveals a format-associated pattern: MC benchmarks concentrate item-related variance (item + model$\times$item DIF) at 68--72\%, while FF benchmarks disperse it to 31--41\% ($n = 5$; permutation $p = 0.10$; item-level $d = 1.73$, benchmark-level CI spans zero); we report this as a descriptive observation. This does not extend to item budgets: HellaSwag (MC, 118 items for $G \geq 0.80$) exceeds ARC (98) and MATH (101), showing format alone does not predict evaluation cost (Figure~\ref{fig:cross_benchmark}). The factorial design is not fully symmetric: ordering applies only to MC, and prompt templates serve format-specific functions, potentially confounding format comparisons. Per-benchmark details---including HellaSwag and ARC exceptions, FF residual analysis, and difficulty dispersion as a variance predictor---are in Appendix~\ref{app:format_details}. All format-associated findings are conditional on binary scoring (\S\ref{sec:binary}).
45	
46	\begin{table}[t]
47	\centering
48	\caption{Format-associated variance structure across five benchmarks (exp-002, 8 models). Variance components (\%) per benchmark; MC = multiple-choice, FF = free-form. Bold = dominant non-residual component. $^\dagger$For MC, reports prompt$\times$item interaction (prompt main effect $<$1\%); for FF, reports prompt main effect (item-conditional interactions absorbed into residual).}
49	\label{tab:cross_benchmark}
50	\scriptsize
51	\begin{tabular}{l rrr rr}
52	\toprule
53	 & \multicolumn{3}{c}{MC} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{FF} \\
54	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-6}
55	 & MMLU & ARC & Hella. & GSM & MATH \\
56	\midrule
57	\textbf{item} & \textbf{37.9} & \textbf{37.9} & 34.3 & 16.9 & 24.0 \\
58	mod.$\times$item & 30.2 & 33.7 & \textbf{36.0} & 14.6 & 17.3 \\
59	model & 2.8 & 3.1 & 5.4 & 10.7 & 8.5 \\
60	prompt$^\dagger$ & 2.5 & 0.4 & 2.3 & 5.4 & 1.0 \\
61	mod.$\times$prmpt & 1.6 & 8.0 & 0.6 & 8.5 & 2.0 \\

Multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure; HellaSwag is the exception where model$\times$item (36.0\%) exceeds item difficulty (34.3\%). Free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) distribute variance across item, model, and interaction components with no single dominant source.}

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The Director is coordinating the Paper Agent to finalize "Reproducibility Budgets for Open-Weight LLM Evaluation: A Generalizability Theory Study of 7–9B Instruction-Tuned Models" for EMNLP 2026 ARR submission (deadline 2026-05-25). This session handled:
   - **Round 10 SF/Minor completion**: SF2, SF3, SF6, SF7(partial), SF8, M2-M5 execution with page budget management
   - **Round 10 residual SF5+SF7 (first pass)**: §4.3 compression + Conclusion expansion
   - **Round 10 补充修复 (CURRENT, pending)**: Further SF7 expansion + MF3 probit quantitative data + optional G_model full-design values

   Hard constraints: main text ≤ 8 pages, 0 critical compile warnings, no overclaims, citations verified.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - G-theory: G-study (variance decomposition) + D-study (reliability projections)
   - σ²_rel = Σ_i σ²_{p×f_i}/n_{f_i} + σ²_residual/Π_i n_{f_i}
   - G(n_p) = n_p·σ²_p / (n_p·σ²_p + σ²_rel)
   - G_model: models as object of measurement, averages 0.79 across 5 benchmarks (0.52 ARC to 0.93 HellaSwag) under 3×3 recommended design
   - Binary bias: binarization inflates residual via Bernoulli p(1-p), making D-study budgets conservative; +36% budget increase (99→135 items)
   - Format-associated variance: MC 68-72% item-related vs FF 31-41%
   - IRT+G composability: IRT-selected 116 items yield G=0.457 single-condition but G=0.895 multi-facet

3. Files and Code Sections:

   - **experiments.tex** (§4.3 compressed)
     - §4.3 reduced from 2 paragraphs of narrative to 1 paragraph + forward reference to Appendix D
     - Current §4.3 main text (line 44):
     ```latex
     We extend to five benchmarks (three MC: MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag; two FF: GSM8K, MATH) using exp-002 (8 models $\times$ BF16 $\times$ 2 temperatures $\times$ 6 prompts $\times$ 6 seeds $\times$ 4 orderings $\times$ 200 items). Table~\ref{tab:cross_benchmark} reveals a format-associated pattern: MC benchmarks concentrate item-related variance (item + model$\times$item DIF) at 68--72\%, while FF benchmarks disperse it to 31--41\% ($n = 5$; permutation $p = 0.10$; item-level $d = 1.73$, benchmark-level CI spans zero); we report this as a descriptive observation. This does not extend to item budgets: HellaSwag (MC, 118 items for $G \geq 0.80$) exceeds ARC (98) and MATH (101), showing format alone does not predict evaluation cost (Figure~\ref{fig:cross_benchmark}). The factorial design is not fully symmetric: ordering applies only to MC, and prompt templates serve format-specific functions, potentially confounding format comparisons. Per-benchmark details---including HellaSwag and ARC exceptions, FF residual analysis, and difficulty dispersion as a variance predictor---are in Appendix~\ref{app:format_details}. All format-associated findings are conditional on binary scoring (\S\ref{sec:binary}).
     ```
     - Table 2: "all others" → "all others (each)" (M3)
     - Table 3: prompt row → "prompt$^\dagger$" with caption adjusted (M2)
     - §4.5: Projection assumptions compressed, adoption gradient merged, "Figure 1 visualizes" removed
     - Robustness checks paragraph compressed

   - **conclusion.tex** (current state):
     ```latex
     A recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design reduces item requirements from 552 to 178 for $G \geq 0.80$, confirmed across five benchmarks under binary scoring; multi-facet designs minimize items when item construction is costly, while single-condition designs suit compute-constrained settings. All budgets assume binary scoring; extending to other model scales and continuous metrics is a priority. Benchmark designers can use D-study projections to size pilot studies before committing to full item development; leaderboard operators can improve ranking stability by averaging over a small number of prompt and seed conditions. Code, data, and a D-study calculator will be released upon publication.
     ```

   - **introduction.tex** (C1 with SF2 addition):

     \item \textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} A recommended 3-prompt $\times$ 3-seed design achieves $G \geq 0.80$ with 178 items (1,602 evaluations per model), a 3.1$\times$ reduction over single-condition designs (552 items); the full 1,296-condition design reduces to 55 items (71,280 evaluations). Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 require no separate control ($<$0.1\% interaction). IRT item selection and G-theory protocol optimization compose: IRT-selected 116 items yield $G = 0.457$ single-condition but $G = 0.895$ multi-facet (Table~\ref{tab:dstudy}).
     ```

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\nAll experiments use open-weight 7--9B parameter models evaluated with vLLM~0.9.2 on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 GPU (96GB) with binary correctness scoring.\n\n\\subsection{Single-Model Baseline}\n\nWe first establish the variance structure within a single model (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) on MMLU to isolate methodological facet contributions before introducing cross-model variation.\n\n\\paragraph{Design.} The G-study crosses six facets: 200 items $\\times$ 3 numerical precisions (BF16, FP16, FP32) $\\times$ 6 prompt templates $\\times$ 6 random seeds $\\times$ 3 temperature levels (0.0, 0.3, 0.7) $\\times$ 4 answer orderings, yielding 259,200 binary observations. Variance components are estimated via Henderson Method~I with bootstrap confidence intervals (1,000 resamples).\n\n\\paragraph{Results.} Under binary scoring, item difficulty (\\texttt{item\\_id}) accounts for 58.4\\% of total variance, with the residual term contributing 23.1\\%. All main facet effects are negligible ($<$1\\% each): precision 0.9\\%, prompt template, temperature, seed, and ordering each $<$0.5\\%. The interesting structure appears in item-conditional interactions: prompt$\\times$item is the largest at 5.5\\%, followed by seed$\\times$item (4.7\\%), precision$\\times$item (3.3\\%), temperature$\\times$item (2.9\\%), and ordering$\\times$item (0.8\\%). This pattern indicates that methodological choices do not shift performance uniformly across items; instead, specific items are differentially sensitive to specific facets---prompt template variation produces the largest item-conditional effect.\n\nThe resulting generalizability coefficient is $G_\\text{item} = 0.936$, indicating that 200 items under multi-facet conditions produce highly reliable rankings. A D-study projection shows $G \\geq 0.80$ is achievable with as few as 55 items in this multi-facet design.\n\nFor text-exact-match scoring (requiring verbatim string matches), the variance structure shifts dramatically: temperature dominates at 79.7\\% of variance, and $G_\\text{item}$ collapses to 0.007. This metric-conditional divergence motivates our exclusive use of binary correctness for the remaining analyses.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Variance}\n\nExtending to eight models (BF16 subset, 460,800 records), Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model} shows that item difficulty drops from 58.4\\% to 37.9\\%, while model$\\times$item interaction emerges at up to 36\\%. The model main effect is 3--5\\% (MC) and 9--11\\% (FF), confirming that models differ primarily in \\emph{which items} they find difficult rather than in overall ability level. Contamination implications are discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:limitations}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Variance decomposition for the cross-model MMLU design (8 models, BF16, 460,800 records). Components estimated via Henderson Method~I. $G_\\text{item} = 0.896$.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lr}\n\\toprule\nComponent & Var.\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{item\\_id} & \\textbf{37.9} \\\\\n\\underline{model$\\times$item} & \\underline{30.2} \\\\\nresidual & 25.0 \\\\\nmodel & 2.8 \\\\\nprompt$\\times$item & 2.5 \\\\\nmodel$\\times$prompt & 1.6 \\\\\nall others (each) & $<$0.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Exploratory: Format-Associated Variance Patterns}\n\\label{sec:format_conditional}\n\nWe extend to five benchmarks (three MC: MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag; two FF: GSM8K, MATH) using exp-002 (8 models $\\times$ BF16 $\\times$ 2 temperatures $\\times$ 6 prompts $\\times$ 6 seeds $\\times$ 4 orderings $\\times$ 200 items). Table~\\ref{tab:cross_benchmark} reveals a format-associated pattern: MC benchmarks concentrate item-related variance (item + model$\\times$item DIF) at 68--72\\%, while FF benchmarks disperse it to 31--41\\% ($n = 5$; permutation $p = 0.10$; item-level $d = 1.73$, benchmark-level CI spans zero); we report this as a descriptive observation. This does not extend to item budgets: HellaSwag (MC, 118 items for $G \\geq 0.80$) exceeds ARC (98) and MATH (101), showing format alone does not predict evaluation cost (Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark}). The factorial design is not fully symmetric: ordering applies only to MC, and prompt templates serve format-specific functions, potentially confounding format comparisons. Per-benchmark details---including HellaSwag and ARC exceptions, FF residual analysis, and difficulty dispersion as a variance predictor---are in Appendix~\\ref{app:format_details}. All format-associated findings are conditional on binary scoring (\\S\\ref{sec:binary}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Format-associated variance structure across five benchmarks (exp-002, 8 models). Variance components (\\%) per benchmark; MC = multiple-choice, FF = free-form. Bold = dominant non-residual component. $^\\dagger$For MC, reports prompt$\\times$item interaction (prompt main effect $<$1\\%); for FF, reports prompt main effect (item-conditional interactions absorbed into residual).}\n\\label{tab:cross_benchmark}\n\\scriptsize\n\\begin{tabular}{l rrr rr}\n\\toprule\n & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{MC} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{FF} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-6}\n & MMLU & ARC & Hella. & GSM & MATH \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{item} & \\textbf{37.9} & \\textbf{37.9} & 34.3 & 16.9 & 24.0 \\\\\nmod.$\\times$item & 30.2 & 33.7 & \\textbf{36.0} & 14.6 & 17.3 \\\\\nmodel & 2.8 & 3.1 & 5.4 & 10.7 & 8.5 \\\\\nprompt$^\\dagger$ & 2.5 & 0.4 & 2.3 & 5.4 & 1.0 \\\\\nmod.$\\times$prmpt & 1.6 & 8.0 & 0.6 & 8.5 & 2.0 \\\\\nresidual & 25.0 & 15.4 & 21.4 & 39.9 & 43.9 \\\\\nall others & $<$1.0 & 1.5 & $<$1.0 & 4.0 & 3.3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_cross_benchmark}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition (Henderson Method~I) across five benchmarks and eight models. Multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure; HellaSwag is the exception where model$\\times$item (36.0\\%) exceeds item difficulty (34.3\\%). Free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) distribute variance across item, model, and interaction components with no single dominant source.}\n\\label{fig:cross_benchmark}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Precision Separability}\n\nUnder binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 are functionally interchangeable ($<$0.1\\% interaction \\cite{yuan2025layercast}); under text-exact-match, a precision$\\times$temperature interaction emerges at 1.3\\%, consistent with \\S4.1. Precision need not be controlled for binary evaluation at BF16/FP16 \\cite{shanmugavelu2024floating}.\n\n\\subsection{Design-Dependent Reproducibility Budgets}\n\nHow many items does a given evaluation design need to achieve a target reliability? We translate variance components into item budgets via D-study projections.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dstudy} compares evaluation designs at the $G \\geq 0.80$ threshold using single-model (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct) MMLU variance components. The multi-facet design (1,296 conditions per item) achieves $G = 0.936$ at 200 items and requires only 55 items for $G \\geq 0.80$. A single-condition design reaches only $G = 0.592$ at 200 items and requires 552 items---a 10.0$\\times$ budget ratio stable across subset sizes (CV = 1.9\\%).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{D-study reproducibility budgets under different evaluation designs (single-model, MMLU). Total evals = items $\\times$ conditions.}\n\\label{tab:dstudy}\n\\scriptsize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcrrrc}\n\\toprule\nDesign & Cond. & $n_{G{\\geq}.80}$ & Total & $G_{200}$ & $G_{116}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nMulti-facet & 1{,}296 & \\textbf{55} & 71{,}280 & \\textbf{.936} & --- \\\\\nSingle-cond. & 1 & 552 & 552 & .592 & .457 \\\\\nIRT+multi & 1{,}296 & --- & 150{,}336 & --- & .895$^\\ddagger$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nIRT baseline & --- & 116 & 116 & --- & .457$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\scriptsize $^\\dagger$\\,PSN-IRT 116 items \\cite{zhou2026irt} under single-condition: $G = 0.457$. $^\\ddagger$\\,Same 116 items under multi-facet: $G = 0.895$.}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\citet{zhou2026irt}'s IRT-selected 116 items yield $G = 0.457$ under single-condition but $G = 0.895$ under multi-facet conditions, demonstrating that IRT item selection and G-theory protocol optimization are orthogonal and complementary. An internal consistency check using four-model subsets confirms projection stability ($|\\Delta G| \\leq 0.029$ for MC, $\\leq 0.065$ for FF; Table~\\ref{tab:forward_pred}).\n\nFor practitioners, the design choice depends on the evaluation bottleneck: the full 1,296-condition design minimizes items (55 items, 71,280 evaluations per model); the recommended 3-prompt $\\times$ 3-seed design balances cost and reliability (178 items, 1,602 evaluations); single-condition designs require 552 items at minimal per-item cost. Multi-facet designs suit settings where item construction is expensive (e.g., expert-crafted benchmarks); more items under fewer conditions suit compute-constrained evaluation (adoption gradient in Appendix~\\ref{app:intermediate_dstudy}). Cross-model projections show budget ratios of 11--22$\\times$ across benchmarks (bootstrap CIs all exceed 10$\\times$; Appendix~\\ref{app:dstudy_cross}), with multi-facet budgets ranging from 71 to 162 items (Table~\\ref{tab:dstudy_cross}).\n\n\\paragraph{Projection assumptions.} Cross-model budgets increase to 93 items because model$\\times$item interaction enters relative error variance. Bootstrap CIs (71--162 items) and LOMO analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}) assess stability. All projections assume the 2024--2025 model generation; the 72B extension (\\S4.6) provides preliminary support.\n\n\\subsection{Preliminary Scale Validation}\n\nWithin-family analysis (Qwen2.5 3B/7B/72B on MMLU) shows qualitatively invariant variance structure: item difficulty dominates at all three scales (73--83\\%, all $G > 0.95$) with consistent facet ranking. The proportional increase at 72B is consistent with the Bernoulli variance ceiling: total variance (0.113) matches $p(1{-}p)$ at grand mean 0.87, compressing all components while item difficulty---the hardest to eliminate---retains the largest share. A crossed 3B$\\times$7B$\\times$72B design yields model$\\times$item = 38.5\\% $>$ item = 35.7\\%, reflecting expected DIF amplification across a 68--87\\% accuracy range ($G_\\text{item} = 0.720$; three models insufficient). The out-of-sample 72B provides preliminary within-family evidence that variance structure is stable across a 24$\\times$ parameter range within Qwen2.5; cross-family generalization remains untested.\n\n\\paragraph{Robustness checks.} Three MMLU resamples yield CV = 3.1\\%; treating models as fixed increases $G$ by 0.077--0.113, confirming conservative budgets (\\S\\ref{sec:model_facet}; seven additional analyses in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}).",
        "numLines": 116,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 116
      }
    },

  },

        "content": "\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nOur eight models share the 2024--2025 instruction-tuning paradigm; temporal correlation may limit exchangeability, and results do not generalize to API-based or base models. Preliminary within-family 72B validation (\\S4.6) suggests stable variance structure across scales, but is limited to a single architecture (Qwen2.5); the Qwen2.5 family shares training recipes and tokenizer, so validating with architecturally distinct families (e.g., Llama-3 70B, Mistral-Large) is necessary before extrapolating budget recommendations to arbitrary model families. The benchmark sample ($n = 5$) limits statistical power for format-associated observations ($p = 0.10$); other formats (code generation, multi-turn dialogue) may differ. All D-study budgets reported in this paper are strictly valid only under binary correctness scoring. Our partial-credit pilot (GSM8K only, single model) shows $G$ drops from 0.76 to 0.55 with $\\rho = 0.26$ for item rankings, suggesting limited transferability to finer-grained scoring rubrics; the partial-credit analysis is limited to a single benchmark and single model, and its generalizability is unknown. Our temperature range (0.0--0.7) does not cover high-temperature regimes ($\\tau > 1.0$) sometimes used in generation tasks. Our design varies few-shot example ordering but not example identity, a known variance source. Results depend on vLLM~0.9.2; different inference engines or vLLM versions may alter CUDA-level nondeterminism patterns. All eight models may have encountered benchmark items during pre-training; differential contamination across architectures could inflate model$\\times$item interaction \\citep{golchin2023contamination, oren2024proving}, though consistent variance structures across three independently constructed MC benchmarks provide indirect evidence against contamination as the sole driver. Contamination affects absolute budget estimates but has limited impact on the multi-facet vs.\\ single-condition budget \\emph{ratio}, since both designs are equally exposed to contaminated items.",

```text
1	\appendix
2	\section{Additional Robustness Analyses}
3	\label{app:robustness}
4	
5	We report nine robustness analyses that verify the stability of the variance decomposition under alternative analytical choices.
6	
7	\paragraph{GLMM complement.} A generalized linear mixed model (GLMM) with marginal logit link produces variance component rankings that perfectly preserve all seven grouped component orderings ($\rho = 1.0$) relative to Henderson Method~I \cite{jiang2018bayesian, tuerlinckx2006glmm}. This confirms that the linear variance decomposition is robust to the binary outcome distribution.
8	
9	\paragraph{Accuracy-range stability.} The \texttt{item\_id} variance share remains stable across subsets with mean accuracy from 0.62 to 0.72 ($r = -0.048$). This rules out distortion from binary floor or ceiling effects at observed accuracy levels.
10	
11	\paragraph{Estimator robustness.} We compare Henderson Method~I with restricted maximum likelihood (REML-EM, 500 iterations, practical convergence $\Delta < 0.003$ percentage points). All variance components differ by $<$0.5 percentage points between estimators. The largest discrepancy occurs on the residual component (0.47pp); all substantive components (item, model$\times$item, model) differ by $<$0.3pp. Henderson Method~I is preferred for our balanced designs because it produces unbiased estimates without distributional assumptions \cite[Ch.~12]{brennan2001generalizability}.
12	
13	\paragraph{Probit simulation.} A probit simulation matched to our 1,296-condition design confirms that Henderson Method~I recovers variance component rankings from dichotomized data (Spearman $\rho = 0.732$ between binary and continuous rankings, excluding residual; \texttt{item\_id} dominant in all 100 replications; top-5 ordering correct in 79\% of replications). Bias analysis by difficulty stratum shows $|\text{bias}| = 0.28$--$0.37$ for items with $p \in [0.5, 0.8]$, where the Bernoulli variance ceiling $p(1{-}p)$ is largest. This analysis covers the observed accuracy range ($p \in [0.3, 0.9]$); extreme-difficulty items ($p < 0.3$ or $p > 0.9$) are rare in our sample ($<$8\% of items) and may exhibit larger deviations. Henderson Method~I absorbs this Bernoulli-inherent variance into the residual, explaining inflated bias near medium difficulty; systematic component ordering is robustly recovered across all replications. Binary scoring inflates residual variance by absorbing the Bernoulli $p(1{-}p)$ ceiling, which enters $\sigma^2_\text{rel}$ and increases D-study item requirements. D-study budgets derived from binary data are therefore conservative: continuous-scored designs would require equal or fewer items for the same $G$ threshold. Null calibration (ratio $= 1.0008$) confirms that this conservatism does not distort relative component magnitudes.
14	
15	\paragraph{CI method comparison.} Bootstrap (1,000 item-level resamples with replacement) and delete-1 jackknife confidence intervals agree closely: width ratios range from 0.96 to 1.04 across all variance components. Bootstrap CIs capture item-sampling variability conditional on these 8 specific models; model-level stability is assessed separately via LOMO (below). The agreement indicates that the dependence structure introduced by item-level bootstrap resampling (which creates $\sim$127 unique items per 200-item resample) does not materially distort interval estimates. Stratified resampling by subject (10 subjects $\times$ 20 items) produces CIs within 4\% of simple item-level bootstrap, confirming that the within-subject dependence structure does not materially affect interval estimates.
16	
17	\paragraph{Facet specification sensitivity.} Treating temperature and ordering as fixed (rather than random) facets changes $G_\text{item}$ by only 0.37 percentage points (all-random: 0.820 vs.\ fixed temperature+ordering: 0.824). The small difference reflects the negligible temperature$\times$item (0.65\%) and ordering$\times$item (0.54\%) variance shares. The random-facet assumption is therefore robust to facet classification.
18	
19	\paragraph{Leave-one-model-out (LOMO).} Across all 40 leave-one-model-out subsets (8 models $\times$ 5 benchmarks), $G_\text{item}$ ranges from 0.833 to 0.919, with CV $\leq$ 1.25\% per benchmark. No single model removal changes $G$ by more than 0.030 (GSM8K, removing Gemma-2-9B). Item-related variance rankings are preserved in all 40 subsets. Table~\ref{tab:lomo_full} reports per-benchmark stability. This supports treating models as exchangeable within the target universe of 7--9B instruction-tuned models (\S\ref{sec:model_facet}).
20	
21	\begin{table}[h]
22	\centering
23	\caption{LOMO stability across five benchmarks (8 models $\times$ 5 benchmarks = 40 subsets).}
24	\label{tab:lomo_full}
25	\scriptsize
26	\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
27	\toprule
28	Benchmark & $G_\text{full}$ & CV (\%) & Max $|\Delta G|$ & Most impactful \\
29	\midrule
30	MMLU & 0.896 & 0.49 & 0.019 & InternLM2.5-7B \\
31	ARC & 0.892 & 0.94 & 0.026 & Llama-3.1-8B \\
32	HellaSwag & 0.872 & 1.09 & 0.028 & Llama-3.1-8B \\
33	GSM8K & 0.863 & 1.25 & 0.030 & Gemma-2-9B \\
34	MATH & 0.888 & 1.20 & 0.029 & InternLM2.5-7B \\
35	\bottomrule
36	\end{tabular}
37	\end{table}
38	
39	\paragraph{Inter-model agreement.} We computed pairwise Cohen's $\kappa$ and Fleiss' $\kappa$ across all model pairs on each benchmark. Mean pairwise $\kappa$ ranges from 0.326 (MATH) to 0.461 (MMLU); Fleiss' $\kappa$ ranges from 0.309 (GSM8K) to 0.456 (MMLU). HellaSwag uses six models ($\kappa = 0.332$) due to missing OLMo and Yi data. The fair-to-moderate agreement independently confirms that the model$\times$item interaction (30--36\% of variance) reflects genuine differential item functioning: models disagree substantially on which items are difficult, consistent with prior DIF findings in educational measurement \cite{holland1993dif, clauser1998dif}.
40	
41	\paragraph{Scoring metric sensitivity.}
42	\label{sec:partial_credit}
43	To probe metric conditionality, we compared binary correctness with step-level partial credit on GSM8K (200 items, 288 conditions, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct). Prompt template variance drops from 25.5\% (binary) to 1.2\% (partial credit), while residual rises from 28.2\% to 46.0\%. Item reliability decreases ($G_\text{item} = 0.553$ vs.\ $0.759$), and item difficulty rankings change substantially ($\rho = 0.26$). Binary scoring amplifies prompt sensitivity by collapsing reasoning quality into pass/fail, confirming that all findings in this paper are metric-conditional.
44	
45	\paragraph{Facet specification sensitivity: random vs.\ fixed model.} Table~\ref{tab:fixed_vs_random} reports per-benchmark generalizability coefficients under random and fixed model specifications. The consistent $\Delta G$ of 0.077--0.113 confirms that random-model budgets are strictly more conservative across all benchmarks.
46	
47	\begin{table}[h]
48	\centering
49	\caption{Generalizability coefficients under random vs.\ fixed model facet specification across five benchmarks.}
50	\label{tab:fixed_vs_random}
```

66	
67	\paragraph{Null calibration.} We generate 100 Bernoulli-distributed datasets with item difficulties matched to the empirical distribution but no systematic facet effects. The observed-to-null variance ratio is 1.0008, confirming that Henderson Method~I does not introduce systematic bias when applied to binary outcomes. The null simulation also verifies that the item variance share (58.4\%) is not an artifact of the Bernoulli $p(1{-}p)$ ceiling: the null produces item variance of 0.08\%, three orders of magnitude below the observed value. Across all 100 replications, the same three components (item, model$\times$item, residual) emerge as the top three (set consistency 100\%), with Kendall's $\tau = 0.81$ between binary and continuous rankings.
68	
69	\paragraph{Reduced crossing validation.} To verify that the reduced crossing in exp-002 (BF16 only $\times$ 2 temperatures) does not introduce confounding, we extracted the corresponding subset from the fully crossed exp-001 design. The generalizability coefficient differs by only 0.0008 ($G = 0.9361$ vs.\ $0.9369$), and all non-precision variance components differ by less than 2 percentage points. The 10pp increase in item variance share (58.4\% $\to$ 68.5\%) is fully explained by the removal of precision-related components ($\sim$4.5\% total), which redistributes across remaining sources. This confirms that the exp-002 design preserves the variance structure of the full crossing.
70	
71	\section{Cross-Model D-Study Projections}
72	\label{app:dstudy_cross}
73	
74	Table~\ref{tab:dstudy_cross} extends the single-model D-study (Table~\ref{tab:dstudy} in main text) to per-benchmark cross-model projections with bootstrap confidence intervals (1,000 item-level resamples, 8 models, 2,304 conditions per item). The efficiency ratio is format-associated: MC benchmarks show 11--13$\times$ (mean 12.0$\times$ [11.6, 12.5]), while FF benchmarks show 21--22$\times$ (mean 21.5$\times$ [20.4, 22.8]).
75

101	The higher item requirements relative to the single-model analysis (55 items in Table~\ref{tab:dstudy}) reflect the additional model$\times$item interaction variance that enters $\sigma^2_\text{rel}$ when models are crossed as a facet. The format-associated ratio pattern---FF benchmarks showing $\sim$2$\times$ the MC ratio---is consistent with the distributed variance structure observed in \S\ref{sec:format_conditional}, where FF benchmarks allocate more variance to model and model$\times$prompt components.
102	
103	\paragraph{Internal consistency check.} Table~\ref{tab:forward_pred} reports consistency checks from four exp-001 models (DeepSeek, Llama, Mistral, Qwen3) to the full eight-model design across all five benchmarks. MC benchmarks show $|\Delta G| \leq 0.029$ ($<$3.5\% error), while FF benchmarks show larger deviations ($|\Delta G| \leq 0.065$, $\sim$7\%) due to limited model diversity in the four-model subset. FF predictions consistently underestimate actual $G$ (conservative); MC predictions show mixed directions with small deviations ($|\Delta G| \leq 0.029$).

129	\label{app:budget_sensitivity}
130	Figure~\ref{fig:budget_sensitivity} shows the effect of $|\Delta G| = 0.035$ estimation error on item budgets across all five benchmarks. The percentage increase is remarkably stable (23.0--24.5\%, mean 23.2\%), because the relative budget adjustment $\Delta n / n$ depends on the $G$-formula ratio rather than on absolute variance components. Absolute $\Delta n$ ranges from 13 (ARC) to 67 (GSM8K), scaling with the baseline budget.

139	\section{Intermediate D-Study Designs}
140	\label{app:intermediate_dstudy}
141	
142	Table~\ref{tab:intermediate_dstudy} shows how reliability improves as evaluation designs increase from single-condition to full multi-facet crossing. The steepest gain occurs at the ``minimal upgrade'' level (2 prompts): $G_{200}$ jumps from 0.592 to 0.692 at 2$\times$ cost. A minimal sufficient design for $G \geq 0.80$ is 3 prompts $\times$ 3 seeds (9 conditions, $G_{200} = 0.818$, $n_{G \geq 0.80} = 178$). Beyond the ``standard'' level (32 conditions), adding orderings provides negligible benefit (ordering$\times$item $<$1\%), confirming that prompt and seed diversity are the primary reliability drivers.
